From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose I should start from the beginning, or at least what feels like the beginning of all this. What brings me in today is... well, it's this feeling that just won't go away. Ever since my friend was killed, I've been living with this constant, heavy mix of emotions. It's like I wake up and there's this immediate wave of guilt, you know? Wondering what I could have done differently, even though logically I know I wasn't there, but the mind just spins and spins. And then there's the fear, this terrible, clinging fear that something awful is going to happen again, just out of the blue, like it did to her. And underneath it all, or maybe right on top of it, is just so much anger. Anger at the world, anger at the unfairness of it all. People kept telling me, you know, my sister, my neighbors, they all said, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" or \"It'll get easier as the months go by.\" But it hasn't. It hasn't eased at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. If anything, it feels like it's settled into my bones, this constant state of just... feeling awful, every single day. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes or what I try to do to distract myself. It's just always there, this dark cloud of guilt and fear and anger, just following me around from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, constantly. It's... it's like I have absolutely no control over my own mind anymore, which is just a terrifying feeling. I'll just be going about my day, trying to run a simple errand, maybe walking down to the pharmacy or the market, and out of nowhere, it just takes over. \n\nIt doesn't even have to be anything significant, either. The most mundane, everyday things will just hijack my brain entirely. For instance, just hearing the deep, heavy growl of a truck shifting gears down the street, or... or the chirping sound of a crosswalk signal changing. Even something as innocent as glancing at one of those walking-tour maps they have set up on the sidewalks downtown. \n\nThe second I encounter one of those things, it's like a switch flips and the whole scene is suddenly put right back into my head. And I mean so sharply, so vividly, that I am right back there. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, right there in my mind's eye, and it happens before I can even try to stop it. I see her foot go down, and my mind is just screaming at her to wait, but the image is already there, playing out in this awful loop. I can't stop the thought, I can't push it away or think about something else. I just stand there, completely frozen, replaying that exact split second. It takes me so long to just catch my breath and realize I'm standing in front of a map in the present day, not back on that street corner. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because I never know when it's going to hit me next. I mean, how do you avoid crosswalks and trucks? You can't. So I'm just constantly at the mercy of these images rushing back in...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do, and it's become quite a burden, to be perfectly honest. I mean, I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection now. I will literally drive an extra fifteen or twenty minutes, taking all these winding back roads through the residential neighborhoods, just to make sure I don't even come within a mile of the place. Even if I just need to get to the grocery store or the post office, which used to be a simple five-minute trip straight down the main avenue, I just... I can't do it. I find myself mapping out these ridiculous, convoluted alternate routes in my head before I even grab my car keys. \n\nAnd it's not just the physical places, either. It's the activities I used to love. I used to be so active, you know, now that I'm retired. But I have completely stopped signing up for most of the walking tours I used to enjoy so much, and I even had to step down from the neighborhood watch and the safety meetings. I just... I cannot bear the conversations. Whenever a group gets together, especially the safety committee, the talk always, inevitably, circles back to what happened there. Someone will bring up pedestrian right-of-ways, or traffic calming measures, or they'll just casually mention her name and the accident, and I feel like the air is just sucked right out of the room. I can't sit there and listen to them dissect it, or offer their little opinions on city planning while I'm sitting there reliving the worst day of my life. It's just too much. So I just stay home. I cancel my RSVPs, I ignore the sign-up sheets... it's just easier to avoid it all entirely than to risk being ambushed by people talking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on the lookout for potential dangers or threats in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is absolutely exhausting, to tell you the truth. Whenever I am anywhere near a road—and I mean, you can't really avoid roads entirely, can you? Even if I take those convoluted back routes I was just telling you about, there is always still traffic. And the very moment I am near it, whether I'm walking on a sidewalk or driving my own car, my eyes are just darting everywhere. I can't just relax and exist in the world anymore. \n\nI watch every single truck that passes by, analyzing how fast it's going, how close it is to the shoulder, whether the driver looks like they might be distracted. I watch the curbs, checking to see if anyone is standing even a fraction of an inch too close to the edge. And the pedestrians... oh, I watch every single pedestrian. It's this overwhelming feeling, doctor, like... like it is my absolute personal responsibility to catch the danger before it happens again. \n\nIt feels like if I just look hard enough, if I just pay enough attention to every little detail around me, I can somehow stop another tragedy from occurring. I'll be clutching my purse so hard my knuckles turn white, just staring at some stranger waiting to cross the street, practically holding my breath until they make it to the other side. I just can't turn it off. I have to monitor everything. Every truck, every curb, every person walking by. It's like I've been appointed the safety officer for the entire world, and if I blink, or if I look away for even a second, someone is going to get killed. I know logically I can't control what happens to other people, but my brain just won't let me let my guard down. I just have to keep scanning, scanning, scanning...\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. In fact, I know I have. It's... it's quite sad, really, when I sit down and think about it. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor. I mean, my calendar was always just bursting with things to do. I used to spend hours, absolutely hours, planning out my garden beds every spring. I'd sketch out where the hydrangeas would go, and how to rotate the perennials... I took such pride in it. And I had my quilting circle with the ladies on Tuesdays, which was always just a wonderful time to catch up and create something beautiful together. We'd have tea, and sometimes someone would bring a lemon cake... \n\nAnd the theater outings! Oh, we used to organize these lovely group trips to the local playhouse, getting dressed up and going out for dinner beforehand. And my book club, too. I used to love our book club meetings. I was always the one who would get into these spirited, happy little arguments about the characters' motivations or the author's pacing. I was so engaged, you know? I felt so alive and connected to my community and my hobbies. \n\nBut now... now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the ones I haven't bothered to erase yet, and I just feel... nothing. It's the strangest sensation. It's like I'm looking at a schedule that belongs to a completely different person. Someone I used to be, a long time ago, before everything changed. The thought of sitting in a circle quilting, or arguing about a novel... it just seems so pointless now. I can't muster up even an ounce of the enthusiasm I used to have. The energy it would take to even pretend to care about those things just feels entirely beyond me. I just sit in my house and look at the garden out the window, and I don't even care that the weeds are starting to take over the flower beds I used to tend so carefully. It's all just... it's just gone. The joy of it is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly what I was just trying to explain... it's exactly that. Those things that were so incredibly important to me, the absolute center of my week—I really can't emphasize enough how much I used to live for them. \n\nLike I was just saying about the garden beds—I used to spend the entire winter pouring over seed catalogs, mapping out the precise color coordination, making sure the soil was just right. It was a passion. And my time quilting with the ladies, and all those lovely theater outings we'd get so dressed up for, and the book club where I'd just happily argue away about the chapters... those weren't just passing hobbies, they were my life. They were what made me, well, me. \n\nBut now? Now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the dates I haven't crossed out, and I feel... absolutely nothing. It's the most bizarre, hollow feeling in the world. I stare at the reminders on my fridge and it truly feels like those appointments, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. It's like reading a stranger's itinerary. The weeds are growing up through the mulch, the books are sitting unread on my nightstand, and I just... I can't find it in myself to care, no matter how much time I spend sitting in my living room thinking about how I really *should* care. I just don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer the person you used to be before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that's it entirely. I mean, I really feel like I'm just a ghost of the woman I was. It's like I was telling you before, about the garden and the quilting and the book club... I was such a vibrant person, doctor. I really was. I was the kind of person who would wake up early on a Saturday just to get to the farmer's market for the best tomatoes, and then I'd spend the afternoon baking, and the evening at the theater. I was always doing something, always engaged with people, always, well, alive. \n\nBut since... since she died, since that awful day, it's like someone just flipped a switch and turned off all the color in my world. I look in the mirror and I see the same face, the same wrinkles I've earned over the years, the same white hair, but the woman looking back at me just isn't the same person who used to plan those elaborate garden beds or argue so passionately about a novel. I look at my calendar, with all those things I used to love written on it, and it really does feel like I'm reading the schedule of a stranger. I just feel so disconnected from that life, from that joy. It's like the woman who loved those things died on that road, too, and I'm just... I'm just what's left behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself from afar?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about things like that, you know, on those television programs or in books where someone has a near-death experience and they float up to the ceiling and look down at the doctors working on them. I always thought that sounded so fantastical, didn't you? But no, I've never felt anything quite like that. \n\nI feel very... stuck in my body, if that makes sense. Too stuck in it, sometimes. I feel the heaviness of it, the way my joints ache a little more these days, the way I just sit in my chair by the window and feel the weight of gravity holding me down. I'm definitely in here. I'm not floating away or watching myself from the corner of the room. I'm just sitting here, feeling hollow. I suppose sometimes I wish I *could* float away, just to get a break from the constant feeling of... well, of not being who I used to be. But no, doctor, I don't have those out-of-body experiences. I'm very much grounded in this reality, as gray and empty as it feels right now. It's just me, sitting here, looking at the garden and not caring about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. I've tried, doctor, I really have. I'll sit there in my armchair, the one with the floral upholstery by the window, and I'll think to myself, \"Surely there must be something.\" But it all just circles back to what I was telling you earlier. It's so frustrating, because I know I *should* look forward to things. \n\nLike, I used to spend hours, absolute hours, planning my garden beds for the spring. It truly feels like those activities, those joys, they belong to someone I used to be. Like I'm looking at a schedule for a completely different woman who just happened to live in my house. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it's just like I was saying a moment ago, isn't it? It's... it's really the same thing. I sit there and I try to find a spark, just a tiny glimmer of the pleasure I used to take in things, but it's just not there. I mean, think about my garden. I used to spend the whole winter, when the ground was frozen solid and there was snow piled up to the windowsills, I would sit at the dining room table with my seed catalogs. I would plan out every square inch of those garden beds. I'd map out where the zinnias would go, and how the climbing roses needed to be trained on the trellis... I took such joy in it. Such immense, profound joy. 

And then there were my Tuesdays with the quilting ladies. Oh, we had such a wonderful time. Martha would always make her famous lemon squares, and we'd sit around the frame, our needles just flying, talking about everything under the sun—our grandchildren, the news, the neighborhood gossip. It was a highlight of my week. And the theater! Getting dressed up, putting on a nice string of pearls and my good wool coat, going out to see a play... and the book club, where we'd debate and laugh until our sides ached... 

But now? Now I look at the calendar hanging there in the kitchen, and I see those things written down—the meetings, the reminders to start the seedlings—and I just... I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's a terrible, heavy kind of nothing. It's exactly as I said before, it feels like those activities, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. I look at the words on the page and I don't feel a drop of pleasure or anticipation. It's just blank. So, no, doctor, I don't think I find pleasure in any of it anymore. It's all just... gone.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose I should start from the beginning, or at least what feels like the beginning of all this. It's like I wake up and there's this immediate wave of guilt, you know? Wondering what I could have done differently, even though logically I know I wasn't there, but the mind just spins and spins. And then there's the fear, this terrible, clinging fear that something awful is going to happen again, just out of the blue, like it did to her. And underneath it all, or maybe right on top of it, is just so much anger. Anger at the world, anger at the unfairness of it all. People kept telling me, you know, my sister, my neighbors, they all said, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" or \"It'll get easier as the months go by.\" But it hasn't. It hasn't eased at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. If anything, it feels like it's settled into my bones, this constant state of just... feeling awful, every single day. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes or what I try to do to distract myself. It's just always there, this dark cloud of guilt and fear and anger, just following me around from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, constantly. It's... it's like I have absolutely no control over my own mind anymore, which is just a terrifying feeling. I'll just be going about my day, trying to run a simple errand, maybe walking down to the pharmacy or the market, and out of nowhere, it just takes over. \n\nIt doesn't even have to be anything significant, either. The most mundane, everyday things will just hijack my brain entirely. For instance, just hearing the deep, heavy growl of a truck shifting gears down the street, or... or the chirping sound of a crosswalk signal changing. Even something as innocent as glancing at one of those walking-tour maps they have set up on the sidewalks downtown. \n\nThe second I encounter one of those things, it's like a switch flips and the whole scene is suddenly put right back into my head. And I mean so sharply, so vividly, that I am right back there. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, right there in my mind's eye, and it happens before I can even try to stop it. I see her foot go down, and my mind is just screaming at her to wait, but the image is already there, playing out in this awful loop. I can't stop the thought, I can't push it away or think about something else. I just stand there, completely frozen, replaying that exact split second. It takes me so long to just catch my breath and realize I'm standing in front of a map in the present day, not back on that street corner. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because I never know when it's going to hit me next. I mean, how do you avoid crosswalks and trucks? You can't. So I'm just constantly at the mercy of these images rushing back in...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do, and it's become quite a burden, to be perfectly honest. I mean, I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection now. I will literally drive an extra fifteen or twenty minutes, taking all these winding back roads through the residential neighborhoods, just to make sure I don't even come within a mile of the place. Even if I just need to get to the grocery store or the post office, which used to be a simple five-minute trip straight down the main avenue, I just... I can't do it. I find myself mapping out these ridiculous, convoluted alternate routes in my head before I even grab my car keys. \n\nAnd it's not just the physical places, either. It's the activities I used to love. I used to be so active, you know, now that I'm retired. But I have completely stopped signing up for most of the walking tours I used to enjoy so much, and I even had to step down from the neighborhood watch and the safety meetings. I just... I cannot bear the conversations. Whenever a group gets together, especially the safety committee, the talk always, inevitably, circles back to what happened there. Someone will bring up pedestrian right-of-ways, or traffic calming measures, or they'll just casually mention her name and the accident, and I feel like the air is just sucked right out of the room. I can't sit there and listen to them dissect it, or offer their little opinions on city planning while I'm sitting there reliving the worst day of my life. It's just too much. So I just stay home. I cancel my RSVPs, I ignore the sign-up sheets... it's just easier to avoid it all entirely than to risk being ambushed by people talking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on the lookout for potential dangers or threats in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is absolutely exhausting, to tell you the truth. Whenever I am anywhere near a road—and I mean, you can't really avoid roads entirely, can you? Even if I take those convoluted back routes I was just telling you about, there is always still traffic. And the very moment I am near it, whether I'm walking on a sidewalk or driving my own car, my eyes are just darting everywhere. I can't just relax and exist in the world anymore. \n\nI watch every single truck that passes by, analyzing how fast it's going, how close it is to the shoulder, whether the driver looks like they might be distracted. I watch the curbs, checking to see if anyone is standing even a fraction of an inch too close to the edge. And the pedestrians... oh, I watch every single pedestrian. It's this overwhelming feeling, doctor, like... like it is my absolute personal responsibility to catch the danger before it happens again. \n\nIt feels like if I just look hard enough, if I just pay enough attention to every little detail around me, I can somehow stop another tragedy from occurring. I'll be clutching my purse so hard my knuckles turn white, just staring at some stranger waiting to cross the street, practically holding my breath until they make it to the other side. I just can't turn it off. I have to monitor everything. Every truck, every curb, every person walking by. It's like I've been appointed the safety officer for the entire world, and if I blink, or if I look away for even a second, someone is going to get killed. I know logically I can't control what happens to other people, but my brain just won't let me let my guard down. I just have to keep scanning, scanning, scanning...\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. In fact, I know I have. It's... it's quite sad, really, when I sit down and think about it. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor. I mean, my calendar was always just bursting with things to do. I used to spend hours, absolutely hours, planning out my garden beds every spring. I'd sketch out where the hydrangeas would go, and how to rotate the perennials... I took such pride in it. And I had my quilting circle with the ladies on Tuesdays, which was always just a wonderful time to catch up and create something beautiful together. We'd have tea, and sometimes someone would bring a lemon cake... \n\nAnd the theater outings! Oh, we used to organize these lovely group trips to the local playhouse, getting dressed up and going out for dinner beforehand. And my book club, too. I used to love our book club meetings. I was always the one who would get into these spirited, happy little arguments about the characters' motivations or the author's pacing. I was so engaged, you know? I felt so alive and connected to my community and my hobbies. \n\nBut now... now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the ones I haven't bothered to erase yet, and I just feel... nothing. It's the strangest sensation. It's like I'm looking at a schedule that belongs to a completely different person. Someone I used to be, a long time ago, before everything changed. The thought of sitting in a circle quilting, or arguing about a novel... it just seems so pointless now. I can't muster up even an ounce of the enthusiasm I used to have. The energy it would take to even pretend to care about those things just feels entirely beyond me. I just sit in my house and look at the garden out the window, and I don't even care that the weeds are starting to take over the flower beds I used to tend so carefully. It's all just... it's just gone. The joy of it is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly what I was just trying to explain... it's exactly that. Those things that were so incredibly important to me, the absolute center of my week—I really can't emphasize enough how much I used to live for them. \n\nLike I was just saying about the garden beds—I used to spend the entire winter pouring over seed catalogs, mapping out the precise color coordination, making sure the soil was just right. It was a passion. And my time quilting with the ladies, and all those lovely theater outings we'd get so dressed up for, and the book club where I'd just happily argue away about the chapters... those weren't just passing hobbies, they were my life. They were what made me, well, me. \n\nBut now? Now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the dates I haven't crossed out, and I feel... absolutely nothing. It's the most bizarre, hollow feeling in the world. I stare at the reminders on my fridge and it truly feels like those appointments, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. It's like reading a stranger's itinerary. The weeds are growing up through the mulch, the books are sitting unread on my nightstand, and I just... I can't find it in myself to care, no matter how much time I spend sitting in my living room thinking about how I really *should* care. I just don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer the person you used to be before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that's it entirely. I mean, I really feel like I'm just a ghost of the woman I was. It's like I was telling you before, about the garden and the quilting and the book club... I was such a vibrant person, doctor. I really was. I was the kind of person who would wake up early on a Saturday just to get to the farmer's market for the best tomatoes, and then I'd spend the afternoon baking, and the evening at the theater. I was always doing something, always engaged with people, always, well, alive. \n\nBut since... since she died, since that awful day, it's like someone just flipped a switch and turned off all the color in my world. I look in the mirror and I see the same face, the same wrinkles I've earned over the years, the same white hair, but the woman looking back at me just isn't the same person who used to plan those elaborate garden beds or argue so passionately about a novel. I look at my calendar, with all those things I used to love written on it, and it really does feel like I'm reading the schedule of a stranger. I just feel so disconnected from that life, from that joy. It's like the woman who loved those things died on that road, too, and I'm just... I'm just what's left behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself from afar?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about things like that, you know, on those television programs or in books where someone has a near-death experience and they float up to the ceiling and look down at the doctors working on them. I always thought that sounded so fantastical, didn't you? But no, I've never felt anything quite like that. \n\nI feel very... stuck in my body, if that makes sense. Too stuck in it, sometimes. I feel the heaviness of it, the way my joints ache a little more these days, the way I just sit in my chair by the window and feel the weight of gravity holding me down. I'm definitely in here. I'm not floating away or watching myself from the corner of the room. I'm just sitting here, feeling hollow. I suppose sometimes I wish I *could* float away, just to get a break from the constant feeling of... well, of not being who I used to be. But no, doctor, I don't have those out-of-body experiences. I'm very much grounded in this reality, as gray and empty as it feels right now. It's just me, sitting here, looking at the garden and not caring about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. I've tried, doctor, I really have. I'll sit there in my armchair, the one with the floral upholstery by the window, and I'll think to myself, \"Surely there must be something.\" But it all just circles back to what I was telling you earlier. It's so frustrating, because I know I *should* look forward to things. \n\nLike, I used to spend hours, absolute hours, planning my garden beds for the spring. I'd have my graph paper out, my colored pencils, making sure the daffodils would bloom right before the tulips, and that the hostas wouldn't overshadow the impatiens. And quilting with the ladies on Tuesdays—that was a staple! We'd have tea and shortbread and just talk for hours while we worked. And the theater outings... oh, we used to get season tickets and dress up so nicely, maybe get dinner beforehand at that little Italian place downtown. And my book club, where I'd just happily argue away about the characters' motivations until we were all laughing. \n\nBut now... I just look at the calendar hanging on the kitchen wall, the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, and I see those things written there. I see \"Book Club, 7 PM\" or \"Quilting at Martha's,\" and I feel... well, I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's just a blankness. It truly feels like those activities, those joys, they belong to someone I used to be. Like I'm looking at a schedule for a completely different woman who just happened to live in my house. I don't look forward to any of it anymore. I just don't have the energy or the... the spark for it. It's all just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you still enjoy or find pleasure in any activities at all?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's just like I was saying a moment ago, isn't it? It's... it's really the same thing. I sit there and I try to find a spark, just a tiny glimmer of the pleasure I used to take in things, but it's just not there. I mean, think about my garden. I used to spend the whole winter, when the ground was frozen solid and there was snow piled up to the windowsills, I would sit at the dining room table with my seed catalogs. I would plan out every square inch of those garden beds. I'd map out where the zinnias would go, and how the climbing roses needed to be trained on the trellis... I took such joy in it. Such immense, profound joy. \n\nAnd then there were my Tuesdays with the quilting ladies. Oh, we had such a wonderful time. Martha would always make her famous lemon squares, and we'd sit around the frame, our needles just flying, talking about everything under the sun—our grandchildren, the news, the neighborhood gossip. It was a highlight of my week. And the theater! Getting dressed up, putting on a nice string of pearls and my good wool coat, going out to see a play... and the book club, where we'd debate and laugh until our sides ached... \n\nBut now? Now I look at the calendar hanging there in the kitchen, and I see those things written down—the meetings, the reminders to start the seedlings—and I just... I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's all just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you still enjoy or find pleasure in any activities at all?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's just like I was saying a moment ago, isn't it? It's... it's really the same thing. I sit there and I try to find a spark, just a tiny glimmer of the pleasure I used to take in things, but it's just not there. I mean, think about my garden. I used to spend the whole winter, when the ground was frozen solid and there was snow piled up to the windowsills, I would sit at the dining room table with my seed catalogs. I would plan out every square inch of those garden beds. I'd map out where the zinnias would go, and how the climbing roses needed to be trained on the trellis... I took such joy in it. Such immense, profound joy. \n\nAnd then there were my Tuesdays with the quilting ladies. Oh, we had such a wonderful time. Martha would always make her famous lemon squares, and we'd sit around the frame, our needles just flying, talking about everything under the sun—our grandchildren, the news, the neighborhood gossip. It was a highlight of my week. And the theater! Getting dressed up, putting on a nice string of pearls and my good wool coat, going out to see a play... and the book club, where we'd debate and laugh until our sides ached... \n\nBut now? Now I look at the calendar hanging there in the kitchen, and I see those things written down—the meetings, the reminders to start the seedlings—and I just... I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's a terrible, heavy kind of nothing. It's exactly as I said before, it feels like those activities, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. I look at the words on the page and I don't feel a drop of pleasure or anticipation. It's just blank. So, no, doctor, I don't think I find pleasure in any of it anymore. It's all just... gone.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any small things that still bring you a bit of happiness or comfort?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to plan my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings, and argue happily at book club, but now I look at those things on the calendar and feel almost nothing, like they belong to someone I used to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you're asking about the small things now, doctor, maybe hoping there's some little loophole or a tiny exception to what I've been saying, but... well, I feel like a terrible broken record, I really do. It all just circles right back to exactly what we've been talking about for the last ten minutes. 

Even the smallest things, the tiny comforts I used to have... they were all wrapped up in those exact same activities. The small comfort of the garden wasn't just the big blooming flowers, it was the feeling of the graph paper under my hand when I used to plan the beds, or the smell of the potting soil. The small happiness in quilting with the ladies wasn't just finishing a blanket, it was the sound of the thread pulling through the fabric, or the taste of the tea we'd drink. The rustle of the playbills at the theater outings, the cozy feeling of the living room when we'd argue happily at book club... those *were* my small things. Those were my comforts. 

And I know I keep saying this, but I don't know how else to explain it to you—I look at those things on the calendar now, all those little moments that made up my life, and I feel almost absolutely nothing. I wish I could give you a different answer. I wish I could say, "Oh yes, watching a bird at the feeder brings me a bit of joy," but it just doesn't. The woman who loved the garden, who loved getting dressed up for the theater and laughing with her friends... she's the one who felt comfort. But now, it truly feels like all of that belongs to someone I used to be. I just sit here and stare at the dates on the wall and there's no comfort to be found in any of it. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but I just can't seem to find a single thing that hasn't been swallowed up by this... this blankness.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose I should start from the beginning, or at least what feels like the beginning of all this. And then there's the fear, this terrible, clinging fear that something awful is going to happen again, just out of the blue, like it did to her. And underneath it all, or maybe right on top of it, is just so much anger. Anger at the world, anger at the unfairness of it all. It hasn't eased at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. If anything, it feels like it's settled into my bones, this constant state of just... feeling awful, every single day. It's just always there, this dark cloud of guilt and fear and anger, just following me around from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. I can't sit there and listen to them dissect it, or offer their little opinions on city planning while I'm sitting there reliving the worst day of my life. It's just too much. So I just stay home. Even if I take those convoluted back routes I was just telling you about, there is always still traffic. And the very moment I am near it, whether I'm walking on a sidewalk or driving my own car, my eyes are just darting everywhere. I can't just relax and exist in the world anymore. \n\nI watch every single truck that passes by, analyzing how fast it's going, how close it is to the shoulder, whether the driver looks like they might be distracted. I watch the curbs, checking to see if anyone is standing even a fraction of an inch too close to the edge. And the pedestrians... oh, I watch every single pedestrian. It's this overwhelming feeling, doctor, like... like it is my absolute personal responsibility to catch the danger before it happens again. \n\nIt feels like if I just look hard enough, if I just pay enough attention to every little detail around me, I can somehow stop another tragedy from occurring. I'll be clutching my purse so hard my knuckles turn white, just staring at some stranger waiting to cross the street, practically holding my breath until they make it to the other side. I just can't turn it off. I have to monitor everything. Every truck, every curb, every person walking by. It's like I've been appointed the safety officer for the entire world, and if I blink, or if I look away for even a second, someone is going to get killed. It's the strangest sensation. It's like I'm looking at a schedule that belongs to a completely different person. Someone I used to be, a long time ago, before everything changed. The thought of sitting in a circle quilting, or arguing about a novel... it just seems so pointless now. I can't muster up even an ounce of the enthusiasm I used to have. The energy it would take to even pretend to care about those things just feels entirely beyond me. I just sit in my house and look at the garden out the window, and I don't even care that the weeds are starting to take over the flower beds I used to tend so carefully. It's all just... it's just gone. I'm very much grounded in this reality, as gray and empty as it feels right now. Even if I take those convoluted back routes I was just telling you about, there is always still traffic. And the very moment I am near it, whether I'm walking on a sidewalk or driving my own car, my eyes are just darting everywhere. I can't just relax and exist in the world anymore. \n\nI watch every single truck that passes by, analyzing how fast it's going, how close it is to the shoulder, whether the driver looks like they might be distracted. I watch the curbs, checking to see if anyone is standing even a fraction of an inch too close to the edge. And the pedestrians... oh, I watch every single pedestrian. It's this overwhelming feeling, doctor, like... like it is my absolute personal responsibility to catch the danger before it happens again. \n\nIt feels like if I just look hard enough, if I just pay enough attention to every little detail around me, I can somehow stop another tragedy from occurring. I'll be clutching my purse so hard my knuckles turn white, just staring at some stranger waiting to cross the street, practically holding my breath until they make it to the other side. I just can't turn it off. I have to monitor everything. Every truck, every curb, every person walking by. It's like I've been appointed the safety officer for the entire world, and if I blink, or if I look away for even a second, someone is going to get killed. I know logically I can't control what happens to other people, but my brain just won't let me let my guard down. I just have to keep scanning, scanning, scanning...\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. In fact, I know I have. It's... it's quite sad, really, when I sit down and think about it. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor. I mean, my calendar was always just bursting with things to do. I used to spend hours, absolutely hours, planning out my garden beds every spring. I'd sketch out where the hydrangeas would go, and how to rotate the perennials... I took such pride in it. And I had my quilting circle with the ladies on Tuesdays, which was always just a wonderful time to catch up and create something beautiful together. We'd have tea, and sometimes someone would bring a lemon cake... \n\nAnd the theater outings! Oh, we used to organize these lovely group trips to the local playhouse, getting dressed up and going out for dinner beforehand. And my book club, too. I used to love our book club meetings. I was always the one who would get into these spirited, happy little arguments about the characters' motivations or the author's pacing. I was so engaged, you know? I felt so alive and connected to my community and my hobbies. \n\nBut now... now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the ones I haven't bothered to erase yet, and I just feel... nothing. It's the strangest sensation. It's like I'm looking at a schedule that belongs to a completely different person. Someone I used to be, a long time ago, before everything changed. The thought of sitting in a circle quilting, or arguing about a novel... it just seems so pointless now. I can't muster up even an ounce of the enthusiasm I used to have. The energy it would take to even pretend to care about those things just feels entirely beyond me. I just sit in my house and look at the garden out the window, and I don't even care that the weeds are starting to take over the flower beds I used to tend so carefully. It's all just... it's just gone. The joy of it is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly what I was just trying to explain... it's exactly that. Those things that were so incredibly important to me, the absolute center of my week—I really can't emphasize enough how much I used to live for them. \n\nLike I was just saying about the garden beds—I used to spend the entire winter pouring over seed catalogs, mapping out the precise color coordination, making sure the soil was just right. It was a passion. And my time quilting with the ladies, and all those lovely theater outings we'd get so dressed up for, and the book club where I'd just happily argue away about the chapters... those weren't just passing hobbies, they were my life. They were what made me, well, me. \n\nBut now? Now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the dates I haven't crossed out, and I feel... absolutely nothing. It's the most bizarre, hollow feeling in the world. I stare at the reminders on my fridge and it truly feels like those appointments, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. It's like reading a stranger's itinerary. The weeds are growing up through the mulch, the books are sitting unread on my nightstand, and I just... I can't find it in myself to care, no matter how much time I spend sitting in my living room thinking about how I really *should* care. I just don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer the person you used to be before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that's it entirely. I mean, I really feel like I'm just a ghost of the woman I was. It's like I was telling you before, about the garden and the quilting and the book club... I was such a vibrant person, doctor. I really was. I was the kind of person who would wake up early on a Saturday just to get to the farmer's market for the best tomatoes, and then I'd spend the afternoon baking, and the evening at the theater. I was always doing something, always engaged with people, always, well, alive. \n\nBut since... since she died, since that awful day, it's like someone just flipped a switch and turned off all the color in my world. I look in the mirror and I see the same face, the same wrinkles I've earned over the years, the same white hair, but the woman looking back at me just isn't the same person who used to plan those elaborate garden beds or argue so passionately about a novel. I look at my calendar, with all those things I used to love written on it, and it really does feel like I'm reading the schedule of a stranger. I just feel so disconnected from that life, from that joy. It's like the woman who loved those things died on that road, too, and I'm just... I'm just what's left behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself from afar?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about things like that, you know, on those television programs or in books where someone has a near-death experience and they float up to the ceiling and look down at the doctors working on them. I always thought that sounded so fantastical, didn't you? But no, I've never felt anything quite like that. \n\nI feel very... stuck in my body, if that makes sense. Too stuck in it, sometimes. I feel the heaviness of it, the way my joints ache a little more these days, the way I just sit in my chair by the window and feel the weight of gravity holding me down. I'm definitely in here. I'm not floating away or watching myself from the corner of the room. I'm just sitting here, feeling hollow. I suppose sometimes I wish I *could* float away, just to get a break from the constant feeling of... well, of not being who I used to be. But no, doctor, I don't have those out-of-body experiences. I'm very much grounded in this reality, as gray and empty as it feels right now. It's just me, sitting here, looking at the garden and not caring about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. I've tried, doctor, I really have. I'll sit there in my armchair, the one with the floral upholstery by the window, and I'll think to myself, \"Surely there must be something.\" But it all just circles back to what I was telling you earlier. It's so frustrating, because I know I *should* look forward to things. \n\nLike, I used to spend hours, absolute hours, planning my garden beds for the spring. I'd have my graph paper out, my colored pencils, making sure the daffodils would bloom right before the tulips, and that the hostas wouldn't overshadow the impatiens. And quilting with the ladies on Tuesdays—that was a staple! We'd have tea and shortbread and just talk for hours while we worked. And the theater outings... oh, we used to get season tickets and dress up so nicely, maybe get dinner beforehand at that little Italian place downtown. And my book club, where I'd just happily argue away about the characters' motivations until we were all laughing. \n\nBut now... I just look at the calendar hanging on the kitchen wall, the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, and I see those things written there. I see \"Book Club, 7 PM\" or \"Quilting at Martha's,\" and I feel... well, I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's just a blankness. It truly feels like those activities, those joys, they belong to someone I used to be. Like I'm looking at a schedule for a completely different woman who just happened to live in my house. I don't look forward to any of it anymore. I just don't have the energy or the... the spark for it. It's all just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you still enjoy or find pleasure in any activities at all?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's just like I was saying a moment ago, isn't it? It's... it's really the same thing. I sit there and I try to find a spark, just a tiny glimmer of the pleasure I used to take in things, but it's just not there. I mean, think about my garden. I used to spend the whole winter, when the ground was frozen solid and there was snow piled up to the windowsills, I would sit at the dining room table with my seed catalogs. I would plan out every square inch of those garden beds. I'd map out where the zinnias would go, and how the climbing roses needed to be trained on the trellis... I took such joy in it. Such immense, profound joy. \n\nAnd then there were my Tuesdays with the quilting ladies. Oh, we had such a wonderful time. Martha would always make her famous lemon squares, and we'd sit around the frame, our needles just flying, talking about everything under the sun—our grandchildren, the news, the neighborhood gossip. It was a highlight of my week. And the theater! Getting dressed up, putting on a nice string of pearls and my good wool coat, going out to see a play... and the book club, where we'd debate and laugh until our sides ached... \n\nBut now? Now I look at the calendar hanging there in the kitchen, and I see those things written down—the meetings, the reminders to start the seedlings—and I just... I feel almost absolutely nothing. It's a terrible, heavy kind of nothing. It's exactly as I said before, it feels like those activities, those passions, they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman. I look at the words on the page and I don't feel a drop of pleasure or anticipation. It's just blank. So, no, doctor, I don't think I find pleasure in any of it anymore. It's all just... gone.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of happiness or comfort?\n\nPatient: I know you're asking about the small things now, doctor, maybe hoping there's some little loophole or a tiny exception to what I've been saying, but... well, I feel like a terrible broken record, I really do. It all just circles right back to exactly what we've been talking about for the last ten minutes. \n\nEven the smallest things, the tiny comforts I used to have... they were all wrapped up in those exact same activities. The small comfort of the garden wasn't just the big blooming flowers, it was the feeling of the graph paper under my hand when I used to plan the beds, or the smell of the potting soil. The small happiness in quilting with the ladies wasn't just finishing a blanket, it was the sound of the thread pulling through the fabric, or the taste of the tea we'd drink. The rustle of the playbills at the theater outings, the cozy feeling of the living room when we'd argue happily at book club... those *were* my small things. Those were my comforts. \n\nAnd I know I keep saying this, but I don't know how else to explain it to you—I look at those things on the calendar now, all those little moments that made up my life, and I feel almost absolutely nothing. I wish I could give you a different answer. I wish I could say, \"Oh yes, watching a bird at the feeder brings me a bit of joy,\" but it just doesn't. The woman who loved the garden, who loved getting dressed up for the theater and laughing with her friends... she's the one who felt comfort. But now, it truly feels like all of that belongs to someone I used to be. I just sit here and stare at the dates on the wall and there's no comfort to be found in any of it. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but I just can't seem to find a single thing that hasn't been swallowed up by this... this blankness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, but I just can't seem to find a single thing that hasn't been swallowed up by this... this blankness.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to plan my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings, and argue happily at book club, but now I look at those things on the calendar and feel almost nothing, like they belong to someone I used to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I... I feel like we are just going in circles, I really do. I don't mean to be difficult, but aren't you just asking me the exact same thing again? Yes. Yes, I am completely unable to experience pleasure from those things. It's exactly what I've been trying to explain for this entire appointment. 

All those things that would normally bring me happiness... I mean, I used to plan my garden beds with such meticulous care, mapping out every little detail for the spring. I used to quilt with the ladies, and we'd have such wonderful afternoons. I used to go to those theater outings all dressed up, and I used to argue happily at my book club until my sides ached. Those *were* the things that normally brought me happiness. That was my life, doctor. 

And now I sit in my kitchen, and I look at those exact things written on my calendar, and I feel almost nothing. It's just a complete void where the joy used to be. It truly feels like all of it—the gardening, the quilting, the theater, the books—like they belong to someone I used to be. A completely different woman who just happens to share my name and live in my house. So, yes, I am unable to experience pleasure from them. I don't know how many different ways I can describe it to you, it's just... the happiness is completely gone.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose I should start from the beginning, or at least what feels like the beginning of all this. It's like I wake up and there's this immediate wave of guilt, you know? Wondering what I could have done differently, even though logically I know I wasn't there, but the mind just spins and spins. And then there's the fear, this terrible, clinging fear that something awful is going to happen again, just out of the blue, like it did to her. And underneath it all, or maybe right on top of it, is just so much anger. Anger at the world, anger at the unfairness of it all. People kept telling me, you know, my sister, my neighbors, they all said, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" or \"It'll get easier as the months go by.\" But it hasn't. It hasn't eased at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. If anything, it feels like it's settled into my bones, this constant state of just... feeling awful, every single day. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes or what I try to do to distract myself. It's just always there, this dark cloud of guilt and fear and anger, just following me around from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to fall asleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Yes, constantly. It's... it's like I have absolutely no control over my own mind anymore, which is just a terrifying feeling. I'll just be going about my day, trying to run a simple errand, maybe walking down to the pharmacy or the market, and out of nowhere, it just takes over. \n\nIt doesn't even have to be anything significant, either. The most mundane, everyday things will just hijack my brain entirely. For instance, just hearing the deep, heavy growl of a truck shifting gears down the street, or... or the chirping sound of a crosswalk signal changing. Even something as innocent as glancing at one of those walking-tour maps they have set up on the sidewalks downtown. \n\nThe second I encounter one of those things, it's like a switch flips and the whole scene is suddenly put right back into my head. And I mean so sharply, so vividly, that I am right back there. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, right there in my mind's eye, and it happens before I can even try to stop it. I see her foot go down, and my mind is just screaming at her to wait, but the image is already there, playing out in this awful loop. I can't stop the thought, I can't push it away or think about something else. I just stand there, completely frozen, replaying that exact split second. It takes me so long to just catch my breath and realize I'm standing in front of a map in the present day, not back on that street corner. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because I never know when it's going to hit me next. I mean, how do you avoid crosswalks and trucks? You can't. Even if I just need to get to the grocery store or the post office, which used to be a simple five-minute trip straight down the main avenue, I just... I can't do it. I find myself mapping out these ridiculous, convoluted alternate routes in my head before I even grab my car keys. \n\nAnd it's not just the physical places, either. It's the activities I used to love. I used to be so active, you know, now that I'm retired. I cannot bear the conversations. Whenever a group gets together, especially the safety committee, the talk always, inevitably, circles back to what happened there. Someone will bring up pedestrian right-of-ways, or traffic calming measures, or they'll just casually mention her name and the accident, and I feel like the air is just sucked right out of the room. I can't sit there and listen to them dissect it, or offer their little opinions on city planning while I'm sitting there reliving the worst day of my life. It's just too much. So I just stay home. I cancel my RSVPs, I ignore the sign-up sheets... it's just easier to avoid it all entirely than to risk being ambushed by people talking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on the lookout for potential dangers or threats in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It is absolutely exhausting, to tell you the truth. Whenever I am anywhere near a road—and I mean, you can't really avoid roads entirely, can you? Even if I take those convoluted back routes I was just telling you about, there is always still traffic. And the very moment I am near it, whether I'm walking on a sidewalk or driving my own car, my eyes are just darting everywhere. I can't just relax and exist in the world anymore. \n\nI watch every single truck that passes by, analyzing how fast it's going, how close it is to the shoulder, whether the driver looks like they might be distracted. I watch the curbs, checking to see if anyone is standing even a fraction of an inch too close to the edge. And the pedestrians... oh, I watch every single pedestrian. It's this overwhelming feeling, doctor, like... like it is my absolute personal responsibility to catch the danger before it happens again. \n\nIt feels like if I just look hard enough, if I just pay enough attention to every little detail around me, I can somehow stop another tragedy from occurring. I'll be clutching my purse so hard my knuckles turn white, just staring at some stranger waiting to cross the street, practically holding my breath until they make it to the other side. I just can't turn it off. I have to monitor everything. Every truck, every curb, every person walking by. It's like I've been appointed the safety officer for the entire world, and if I blink, or if I look away for even a second, someone is going to get killed. I know logically I can't control what happens to other people, but my brain just won't let me let my guard down. I just have to keep scanning, scanning, scanning...\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. In fact, I know I have. It's... it's quite sad, really, when I sit down and think about it. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor. I mean, my calendar was always just bursting with things to do. I used to spend hours, absolutely hours, planning out my garden beds every spring. I'd sketch out where the hydrangeas would go, and how to rotate the perennials... I took such pride in it. And I had my quilting circle with the ladies on Tuesdays, which was always just a wonderful time to catch up and create something beautiful together. We'd have tea, and sometimes someone would bring a lemon cake... \n\nAnd the theater outings! Oh, we used to organize these lovely group trips to the local playhouse, getting dressed up and going out for dinner beforehand. And my book club, too. I used to love our book club meetings. I was always the one who would get into these spirited, happy little arguments about the characters' motivations or the author's pacing. I was so engaged, you know? I felt so alive and connected to my community and my hobbies. \n\nBut now... now I just look at those things written on the calendar, the ones I haven't bothered to erase yet, and I just feel... nothing. It's the strangest sensation. It's like I'm looking at a schedule that belongs to a completely different person. Someone I used to be, a long time ago, before everything changed. The thought of sitting in a circle quilting, or arguing about a novel... it just seems so pointless now. I can't muster up even an ounce of the enthusiasm I used to have. The energy it would take to even pretend to care about those things just feels entirely beyond me. I just sit in my house and look at the garden out the window, and I don't even care that the weeds are starting to take over the flower beds I used to tend so carefully. It's all just... it's just gone. The joy of it is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, that's exactly what I was just trying to explain... it's exactly that. A completely different woman. It's like reading a stranger's itinerary. The weeds are growing up through the mulch, the books are sitting unread on my nightstand, and I just... I can't find it in myself to care, no matter how much time I spend sitting in my living room thinking about how I really *should* care. I just don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer the person you used to be before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that's it entirely. I just feel so disconnected from that life, from that joy. It's like the woman who loved those things died on that road, too, and I'm just... I'm just what's left behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself from afar?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about things like that, you know, on those television programs or in books where someone has a near-death experience and they float up to the ceiling and look down at the doctors working on them. I always thought that sounded so fantastical, didn't you? But no, I've never felt anything quite like that. \n\nI feel very... stuck in my body, if that makes sense. Too stuck in it, sometimes. I'm not floating away or watching myself from the corner of the room. I'm just sitting here, feeling hollow. I'm very much grounded in this reality, as gray and empty as it feels right now. It's just me, sitting here, looking at the garden and not caring about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying?\n\nPatient: Well, I... And quilting with the ladies on Tuesdays—that was a staple! We'd have tea and shortbread and just talk for hours while we worked. And the theater outings... oh, we used to get season tickets and dress up so nicely, maybe get dinner beforehand at that little Italian place downtown. And my book club, where I'd just happily argue away about the characters' motivations until we were all laughing. \n\nBut now... I just look at the calendar hanging on the kitchen wall, the one with the Thomas Kinkade paintings, and I see those things written there. It truly feels like those activities, those joys, they belong to someone I used to be. Like I'm looking at a schedule for a completely different woman who just happened to live in my house. I don't look forward to any of it anymore. I just don't have the energy or the... the spark for it. It's all just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you still enjoy or find pleasure in any activities at all?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's just like I was saying a moment ago, isn't it? It's... it's really the same thing. I sit there and I try to find a spark, just a tiny glimmer of the pleasure I used to take in things, but it's just not there. I mean, think about my garden. I used to spend the whole winter, when the ground was frozen solid and there was snow piled up to the windowsills, I would sit at the dining room table with my seed catalogs. I would plan out every square inch of those garden beds. I'd map out where the zinnias would go, and how the climbing roses needed to be trained on the trellis... I took such joy in it. I look at the words on the page and I don't feel a drop of pleasure or anticipation. It's just blank. So, no, doctor, I don't think I find pleasure in any of it anymore. It's all just... gone.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of happiness or comfort?\n\nPatient: I know you're asking about the small things now, doctor, maybe hoping there's some little loophole or a tiny exception to what I've been saying, but... well, I feel like a terrible broken record, I really do. It all just circles right back to exactly what we've been talking about for the last ten minutes. \n\nEven the smallest things, the tiny comforts I used to have... they were all wrapped up in those exact same activities. The small comfort of the garden wasn't just the big blooming flowers, it was the feeling of the graph paper under my hand when I used to plan the beds, or the smell of the potting soil. The small happiness in quilting with the ladies wasn't just finishing a blanket, it was the sound of the thread pulling through the fabric, or the taste of the tea we'd drink. The rustle of the playbills at the theater outings, the cozy feeling of the living room when we'd argue happily at book club... those *were* my small things. Those were my comforts. \n\nAnd I know I keep saying this, but I don't know how else to explain it to you—I look at those things on the calendar now, all those little moments that made up my life, and I feel almost absolutely nothing. I wish I could give you a different answer. I wish I could say, \"Oh yes, watching a bird at the feeder brings me a bit of joy,\" but it just doesn't. The woman who loved the garden, who loved getting dressed up for the theater and laughing with her friends... she's the one who felt comfort. But now, it truly feels like all of that belongs to someone I used to be. I just sit here and stare at the dates on the wall and there's no comfort to be found in any of it.
